Now let's think about the `EvaluationHandle` internals.

#### Handle state design

Option A: `EvaluationHandle` wraps `Gc<HandleInner>` where `HandleInner` contains:
```rust
struct HandleInner {
    parent: Option<EvaluationHandle>,  // traceable
    state: GcRefCell<HandleState>,
}
struct HandleState {
    cancelled: bool,
    reason: Option<JsValue>,  // None = cancelled without custom reason
}
```
But `cancelled` and `reason` in a `GcRefCell` - both mutable. We need atomicity for first-wins? Since single-threaded (Boa is single-threaded per
context), `GcRefCell` is fine. But `cancel_with_reason` must be callable from within jobs/callbacks that might be inside a borrow? We only borrow
briefly.

However, there's a subtlety: `cancel`/`cancel_with_reason` return `bool` indicating whether that call performed the first effective cancellation. For
a handle that is already cancelled (own or inherited), `cancel` returns false. For a handle not cancelled, calling `cancel` sets its own cancelled
state and returns true. If parent is already cancelled, then `is_cancelled` is true and `cancel` should return false (because it didn't perform the
*first* effective cancellation - the parent already did). Also if the child has its own reason already, returns false.

Now, `cancellation_reason(context) -> Option<JsValue>`:
- If self.cancelled is true: return Some(self.reason.clone() or construct AbortError).
- Else walk ancestors: find nearest ancestor with `cancelled` true, return its reason.
- Else None.

But wait: "For descendant handles, cancellation_reason(context) must surface inherited ancestor cancellation reason unless the descendant already has
its own first effective reason." This matches.

Now, what if a descendant cancels first with reason R1, then the parent cancels with R2. The descendant's `is_cancelled` true with own reason R1. The
parent is cancelled with R2. For the descendant, `cancellation_reason` returns R1 (own). For the parent's *other* descendants, they inherit R2. Good.

But requirement 1: "Parent cancellation must cascade to all descendant handles." If we walk ancestors, when parent is cancelled, descendants'
`is_cancelled` becomes true. Good.

Now, should cancelling a child set a flag on the child only? Yes. And "child cancellation must not cancel its parent" - walking ancestors only checks
upward, so cancelling child doesn't affect parent. Good.

Now, first-wins per node: use `cancelled` flag + `reason: Option<JsValue>`. If `cancel_with_reason(r)` first call sets `cancelled = true` and `reason
= Some(r)`, returns true. Subsequent calls return false and don't change. If `cancel()` first call sets `cancelled = true` and `reason = None`,
returns true. Later `cancel_with_reason(r)` returns false and doesn't replace (reason stays None - meaning default AbortError). Later `cancel()`
returns false.

But there's a question: if `cancel_with_reason` is called on a descendant whose parent is already cancelled (so descendant is already effectively
cancelled), should it still set descendant's own reason and return true? Requirement 3: "the first effective cancellation determines its reason and
later attempts cannot replace it." Since the parent's cancellation was first *effective*, the descendant's `cancel_with_reason` should not be the
first effective cancellation - it should return false. And `cancellation_reason` for the descendant should surface inherited ancestor reason "unless
the descendant already has its own first effective reason" - since the descendant's own cancellation was not first effective, it should not surface
its own reason. So if parent is cancelled, a child's `cancel_with_reason(R)` should return false and NOT set the child's own reason. This aligns with
first-wins at the lineage level.

But wait - what if the child is cancelled first (own effective), and then parent cancelled later? The child has its own first effective reason, so it
surfaces its own. Parent's cancellation is first effective for the parent but not for the child (child already had its own effective cancellation).
Good.

So the rule: `cancel`/`cancel_with_reason` on a handle returns true only if neither self nor any ancestor is already cancelled. If returns true, sets
self.cancelled and self.reason. If returns false, does nothing (doesn't set own reason).

This means `is_cancelled` = self.cancelled || any ancestor cancelled. And `own_effective` = self.cancelled (which only gets set when it was the first
effective cancellation along its lineage).

Wait, but consider: child C cancels first (own effective). Then grandchild G (child of C) tries `cancel_with_reason(R)`. C is already cancelled, so
G's cancel returns false and G's own reason not set. Good. `cancellation_reason(G)` walks: G not cancelled, C cancelled -> returns C's reason. Good.

Now consider: parent P not cancelled, child C cancels first (C.cancelled=true, reason R1). Then parent P cancels (P.cancelled=true, reason R2). Now
C's `cancellation_reason` returns C's own R1 (own effective). P's other child D (not cancelled) inherits P's R2. This matches "unless the descendant
already has its own first effective reason".

Now, another subtle case: P not cancelled, C cancels with reason R1. C has child G. G cancels? G's parent C is already cancelled, so G's cancel
returns false and no own reason. `cancellation_reason(G)` returns C's R1. Good.

So the implementation is straightforward.

But we need to handle the "reason" being stored as `JsValue` and constructed lazily for no-reason case. Let's decide: store `reason: Option<JsValue>`
where `None` means "cancelled without custom reason" and `Some(v)` means custom reason. `cancellation_reason(context)` for a node with
`cancelled=true` and `reason=None` constructs `make_abort_error(context)` each time (or caches it). Requirement says "must produce an Error-like value
whose string contains AbortError." It doesn't require identity across calls. Caching would be nice but requires context to create, and `cancel()`
doesn't have context. We could cache lazily in the `GcRefCell` but the borrow makes it tricky. Simpler: construct fresh each time. But then if a job's
handle is cancelled without reason and `cancellation_reason` is called, it returns a new object each time - acceptable per spec? The requirement just
says produce an Error-like value. Let's keep it simple: construct fresh each call.

But wait: for `Module::evaluate_with_evaluation` rejection reason, "must reject with the same cancellation reason value that cancelled the handle." If
cancelled without reason, the rejection reason is the AbortError constructed. That's fine.

Now, about storing `JsValue` in `GcRefCell`: Since `HandleInner` derives `Trace`, `GcRefCell<Option<JsValue>>` will be traced. Good.

But `EvaluationHandle` contains `Gc<HandleInner>`, and `HandleInner.parent` is `Option<EvaluationHandle>` which itself is `Gc<HandleInner>` - this
creates a recursive type. Rust allows recursion through `Gc` (heap pointer). Deriving Trace on `EvaluationHandle` (which contains `Gc<HandleInner>`)
and `HandleInner` (which contains `Option<EvaluationHandle>`) should work. Let me verify `Gc` implements Trace. Let's look at boa_gc.

type ErasedWeakMapBoxPointer = NonNull<dyn ErasedWeakMapBox>;

thread_local!(static GC_DROPPING: Cell<bool> = const { Cell::new(false) });
thread_local!(static BOA_GC: RefCell<BoaGc> = RefCell::new( BoaGc {
    config: GcConfig::default(),
    runtime: GcRuntimeData::default(),
    strongs: Vec::default(),
    weaks: Vec::default(),
    weak_maps: Vec::default(),
}));

#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy)]
struct GcConfig {
    /// The threshold at which the garbage collector will trigger a collection.
    threshold: usize,
    /// The percentage of used space at which the garbage collector will trigger a collection.
    used_space_percentage: usize,
}

// Setting the defaults to an arbitrary value currently.
//
// TODO: Add a configure later
impl Default for GcConfig {
    fn default() -> Self {
        Self {
            // Start at 1MB, the nursary size for V8 is ~1-8MB and SM can be up to 16MB
            threshold: 1_048_576,
            used_space_percentage: 70,
        }
    }
}

#[derive(Default, Debug, Clone, Copy)]
struct GcRuntimeData {
    collections: usize,
    bytes_allocated: usize,
}

#[derive(Debug)]
struct BoaGc {
    config: GcConfig,
    runtime: GcRuntimeData,
    strongs: Vec<GcErasedPointer>,
    weaks: Vec<EphemeronPointer>,
    weak_maps: Vec<ErasedWeakMapBoxPointer>,
}

impl Drop for BoaGc {
    fn drop(&mut self) {
        Collector::dump(self);
    }
}

// Whether or not the thread is currently in the sweep phase of garbage collection.
// During this phase, attempts to dereference a `Gc<T>` pointer will trigger a panic.
/// `DropGuard` flags whether the Collector is currently running `Collector::sweep()` or `Collector::dump()`
///
/// While the `DropGuard` is active, all `GcBox`s must not be dereferenced or accessed as it could cause Undefined Behavior
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
struct DropGuard;

impl DropGuard {
    fn new() -> Self {
        GC_DROPPING.with(|dropping| dropping.set(true));
        Self
    }
}

impl Drop for DropGuard {
    fn drop(&mut self) {
        GC_DROPPING.with(|dropping| dropping.set(false));
    }
}

/// Returns `true` if it is safe for a type to run [`Finalize::finalize`].
#[must_use]
#[inline]
pub fn finalizer_safe() -> bool {
    GC_DROPPING.with(|dropping| !dropping.get())

/// Zero sized struct that is used to ensure that we do not call trace methods,
/// call its finalization method or drop it.
///
/// This can only happen if we are accessing a [`GcErasedPointer`] directly which is a bug.
/// Panics if any of it's methods are called.
///
/// Note: Accessing the [`crate::internals::GcHeader`] of [`GcErasedPointer`] is fine.
pub(crate) struct NonTraceable(());

impl Finalize for NonTraceable {
    fn finalize(&self) {
        unreachable!()
    }
}

unsafe impl Trace for NonTraceable {
    unsafe fn trace(&self, _tracer: &mut Tracer) {
        unreachable!()
    }
    unsafe fn trace_non_roots(&self) {
        unreachable!()
    }
    fn run_finalizer(&self) {
        unreachable!()
    }
}

impl Drop for NonTraceable {
    fn drop(&mut self) {
        unreachable!()
    }
}

/// A type erased [`Gc<T>`] pointer type.
#[repr(transparent)]
pub struct GcErased {
    inner: Gc<NonTraceable>,
}

impl GcErased {
    /// Convert a [`Gc<T>`] into a type erased [`GcErased`].
    #[inline]
    #[must_use]
    pub fn new<T: Trace>(gc: Gc<T>) -> Self {
        let inner_ptr = gc.inner_ptr;
        std::mem::forget(gc);

        Self {
            inner: Gc {
                inner_ptr: inner_ptr.cast(),
                marker: PhantomData,
            },
        }
    }

    /// Returns `true` if the two [`GcErased`]s point to the same allocation.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn ptr_eq(this: &Self, other: &Self) -> bool {
        Gc::ptr_eq(&this.inner, &other.inner)
    }

    /// Returns the [`TypeId`] of the inner type.
    #[inline]
    #[must_use]
    pub fn type_id(&self) -> TypeId {
        Gc::type_id(&self.inner)
    }

    /// Returns true if the inner type is the same as `T`.
    #[inline]
    #[must_use]
    pub fn is<T: Trace + 'static>(&self) -> bool {
        Gc::is::<T>(&self.inner)
    }

    /// Returns [`Some`] `Gc<T>` if it is of type `T`, or [`None`] if it isn’t.
    #[inline]
    #[must_use]
    pub fn downcast<T: Trace + 'static>(self) -> Option<Gc<T>> {
        Gc::downcast::<T>(self.inner)
    }

`Gc<T: Trace>` implements `Trace`, `Finalize`, `Clone`, `Debug`. Great.

Now, let's think about whether to use `Gc` vs `Rc`. Since `EvaluationHandle` needs `Trace`, `Gc` is the right choice. But there is a concern:
`Gc::new` requires the GC to be initialized (which happens when Context is created). `new_evaluation_handle` has `&Context`/`&mut Context`, so fine.
`child` also has `&mut Context` (or `&Context`). Actually the public method `EvaluationHandle::child` might take `&self, context: &mut Context` - we
need context to allocate the `Gc` child node. Good.

impl<T: Trace + ?Sized> Gc<T> {
    /// Constructs a new `Gc<T>` with the given value.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn new(value: T) -> Self
    where
        T: Sized,
    {
        // Create GcBox and allocate it to heap.
        //
        // Note: Allocator can cause Collector to run
        let inner_ptr = Allocator::alloc_gc(GcBox::new(value));

        Self {
            inner_ptr,
            marker: PhantomData,
        }
    }

    /// Constructs a new `Gc<T>` while giving you a `WeakGc<T>` to the allocation, to allow
    /// constructing a T which holds a weak pointer to itself.
    ///
    /// Since the new `Gc<T>` is not fully-constructed until `Gc<T>::new_cyclic` returns, calling
    /// [`upgrade`][WeakGc::upgrade]  on the weak reference inside the closure will fail and result
    /// in a `None` value.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn new_cyclic<F>(data_fn: F) -> Self
    where
        F: FnOnce(&WeakGc<T>) -> T,
        T: Sized,
    {
        // SAFETY: The newly allocated ephemeron is only live here, meaning `Ephemeron` is the
        // sole owner of the allocation after passing it to `from_raw`, making this operation safe.
        let weak = unsafe {
            Ephemeron::from_raw(Allocator::alloc_ephemeron(EphemeronBox::new_empty())).into()
        };

        let gc = Self::new(data_fn(&weak));

        // SAFETY:
        // - `as_mut`: `weak` is properly initialized by `alloc_ephemeron` and cannot escape the
        //   `unsafe` block.
        // - `set_kv`: `weak` is a newly created `EphemeronBox`, meaning it isn't possible to
        //   collect it since `weak` is still live.
        unsafe { weak.inner().inner_ptr().as_mut().set(&gc, ()) }

        gc
    }

    /// Consumes the `Gc`, returning a wrapped raw pointer.
    ///
    /// To avoid a memory leak, the pointer must be converted back to a `Gc` using [`Gc::from_raw`].
    #[must_use]
    pub fn into_raw(this: Self) -> NonNull<GcBox<T>> {
        let ptr = this.inner_ptr();
        std::mem::forget(this);
        ptr
    }

    /// Returns `true` if the two `Gc`s point to the same allocation.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn ptr_eq<U: Trace + ?Sized>(this: &Self, other: &Gc<U>) -> bool {
        std::ptr::addr_eq(this.inner(), other.inner())
    }

    /// Constructs a `Gc<T>` from a raw pointer.
    ///
    /// The raw pointer must have been returned by a previous call to [`Gc<U>::into_raw`][Gc::into_raw]
    /// where `U` must have the same size and alignment as `T`.
    ///
    /// # Safety
    ///

#[derive(Default)]
pub struct ContextBuilder {
    interner: Option<Interner>,
    host_hooks: Option<Rc<dyn HostHooks>>,
    clock: Option<Rc<dyn Clock>>,
    job_executor: Option<Rc<dyn JobExecutor>>,
    module_loader: Option<Rc<dyn DynModuleLoader>>,

    icu: Option<icu::IntlProvider>,
    #[cfg(feature = "temporal")]
    timezone_provider: Option<Box<dyn TimeZoneProvider>>,
    #[cfg(feature = "fuzz")]
    instructions_remaining: usize,
}

impl std::fmt::Debug for ContextBuilder {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut std::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        #[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug)]
        struct JobExecutor;
        #[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug)]
        struct HostHooks;
        #[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug)]
        struct Clock;
        #[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug)]
        struct ModuleLoader;

        let mut out = f.debug_struct("ContextBuilder");

        out.field("interner", &self.interner)
            .field("host_hooks", &self.host_hooks.as_ref().map(|_| HostHooks))
            .field("clock", &self.clock.as_ref().map(|_| Clock))
            .field(
                "job_executor",
                &self.job_executor.as_ref().map(|_| JobExecutor),
            )
            .field(
                "module_loader",
                &self.module_loader.as_ref().map(|_| ModuleLoader),
            )
            .field("can_block", &self.can_block);

        #[cfg(feature = "intl")]
        out.field("icu", &self.icu);

        #[cfg(feature = "temporal")]
        out.field(
            "timezone_provider",
            &self.timezone_provider.as_ref().map(|_| "TimeZoneProvider"),
        );

        #[cfg(feature = "fuzz")]
        out.field("instructions_remaining", &self.instructions_remaining);

        out.finish()
    }
}

impl ContextBuilder {
    /// Creates a new [`ContextBuilder`] with a default empty [`Interner`]
    /// and a default `BoaProvider` if the `intl` feature is enabled.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn new() -> Self {
        Self::default()
    }

    /// Initializes the context [`Interner`] to the provided interner.
    ///
    /// This is useful when you want to initialize an [`Interner`] with
    /// a collection of words before parsing.
    #[must_use]
    #[allow(clippy::missing_const_for_fn)]
    pub fn interner(mut self, interner: Interner) -> Self {
        self.interner = Some(interner);
        self
    }

    /// Provides a [`BufferProvider`] data provider to the [`Context`].
    ///
    /// This function is only available if the `intl` feature is enabled.
    ///
    /// # Additional considerations
    ///
    /// If the data was generated using `icu_datagen`, make sure that the deduplication strategy is
    /// not set to [`Maximal`]. Otherwise, `icu_datagen` will delete base locales such as "en" from
    /// the list of supported locales if the required data for "en" is the same as "und".
    /// We recommend [`RetainBaseLanguages`] as a nice default, which will only deduplicate locales
    /// if the deduplication target is not "und".
    ///
    /// # Errors
    ///
    /// This returns `Err` if the provided provider doesn't have the required locale information
    /// to construct common tools used through `Intl`. Note that this doesn't
    /// mean that the provider will successfully construct all `Intl` services; that check is made
    /// until the creation of an instance of a service.
    ///
    /// [`Maximal`]: https://docs.rs/icu_datagen/latest/icu_datagen/enum.DeduplicationStrategy.html#variant.Maximal
    /// [`RetainBaseLanguages`]: https://docs.rs/icu_datagen/latest/icu_datagen/enum.DeduplicationStrategy.html#variant.RetainBaseLanguages
    /// [`BufferProvider`]: icu_provider::buf::BufferProvider
    #[cfg(feature = "intl")]
    pub fn icu_buffer_provider<
        T: icu_provider::prelude::DynamicDryDataProvider<icu_provider::prelude::BufferMarker>
            + 'static,
    >(
        mut self,
        provider: T,
    ) -> Result<Self, IcuError> {
        self.icu = Some(icu::IntlProvider::try_new_buffer(provider));
        Ok(self)
    }

    /// Set the [`timezone_provider::provider::TimeZoneProvider`] that should be used to source
    /// time zone data.
    ///
    /// ## Default
    ///
    /// If no time zone provider is provided, a compiled time zone provider will be used
    /// which includes the time zone data in the binary. This may increase binary sizes
    /// by up to 200 Kb.
    #[cfg(feature = "temporal")]
    #[must_use]
    pub fn timezone_provider<T: TimeZoneProvider + 'static>(mut self, provider: T) -> Self {
        self.timezone_provider = Some(Box::new(provider));
        self
    }

    /// Initializes the [`HostHooks`] for the context.
    ///
    /// [`Host Hooks`]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-host-hooks-summary
    #[must_use]
    pub fn host_hooks<H: HostHooks + 'static>(mut self, host_hooks: Rc<H>) -> Self {
        self.host_hooks = Some(host_hooks);
        self
    }

    /// Initializes the [`Clock`] for the context.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn clock<C: Clock + 'static>(mut self, clock: Rc<C>) -> Self {
        self.clock = Some(clock);
        self
    }

    /// Initializes the [`JobExecutor`] for the context.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn job_executor<Q: JobExecutor + 'static>(mut self, job_executor: Rc<Q>) -> Self {
        self.job_executor = Some(job_executor);
        self
    }

    /// Initializes the [`ModuleLoader`] for the context.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn module_loader<M: ModuleLoader + 'static>(mut self, module_loader: Rc<M>) -> Self {
        self.module_loader = Some(module_loader);
        self
    }

    /// [`AgentCanSuspend ( )`][spec] aka `[[CanBlock]]`
    ///
    /// Defines if this context can be suspended by calls to the [`Atomics.wait`][wait] function.
    ///
    /// # Note
    ///
    /// By the specification, multiple agents cannot share the same thread if any of them has its
    /// `[[CanBlock]]` field set to true. The builder will verify at build time that all contexts on
    /// the current thread fulfill this requisite.
    ///
    /// [spec]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-agentcansuspend
    /// [wait]: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/JavaScript/Reference/Global_Objects/Atomics/wait
    #[must_use]
    pub const fn can_block(mut self, can_block: bool) -> Self {
        self.can_block = can_block;
        self
    }

    /// Specifies the number of instructions remaining to the [`Context`].
    ///
    /// This function is only available if the `fuzz` feature is enabled.
    #[cfg(feature = "fuzz")]
    #[must_use]
    pub const fn instructions_remaining(mut self, instructions_remaining: usize) -> Self {
        self.instructions_remaining = instructions_remaining;
        self
    }

    /// Builds a new [`Context`] with the provided parameters, and defaults
    /// all missing parameters to their default values.
    // TODO: try to use a custom error here, since most of the `JsError` APIs
    // require having a `Context` in the first place.
    pub fn build(self) -> JsResult<Context> {
        if self.can_block {
            if CANNOT_BLOCK_COUNTER.get() > 0 {
                return Err(JsNativeError::typ()
                    .with_message(
                        "a context that can block must be the only active context in its current thread",
                    )
                    .into());
            }
        } else {
            CANNOT_BLOCK_COUNTER.set(CANNOT_BLOCK_COUNTER.get() + 1);
        }

        let root_shape = RootShape::default();

        let host_hooks = self.host_hooks.unwrap_or(Rc::new(DefaultHooks));
        let clock = self.clock.unwrap_or_else(|| Rc::new(StdClock::new()));
        let realm = Realm::create(host_hooks.as_ref(), &root_shape)?;
        let vm = Vm::new(realm);

        let module_loader: Rc<dyn DynModuleLoader> = if let Some(loader) = self.module_loader {
            loader
        } else if let Ok(loader) = SimpleModuleLoader::new(Path::new(".")) {
            Rc::new(loader)
        } else {
            Rc::new(IdleModuleLoader)
        };

        let job_executor = self
            .job_executor
            .unwrap_or_else(|| Rc::new(SimpleJobExecutor::new()));

        let mut context = Context {
            interner: self.interner.unwrap_or_default(),
            vm,
            strict: false,
            #[cfg(feature = "temporal")]
            timezone_provider: if let Some(provider) = self.timezone_provider {
                provider
            } else {
                Box::new(ZeroCompiledTzdbProvider::default())
            },
            #[cfg(feature = "intl")]
            intl_provider: if let Some(icu) = self.icu {
                icu
            } else {
                cfg_if::cfg_if! {
                    if #[cfg(feature = "intl_bundled")] {
                        icu::IntlProvider::try_new_buffer(boa_icu_provider::buffer())
                    } else {
                        return Err(JsNativeError::typ()
                            .with_message("missing Intl provider for context")
                            .into()
                        );
                    }
                }
            },
            #[cfg(feature = "fuzz")]
            instructions_remaining: self.instructions_remaining,
            kept_alive: Vec::new(),
            host_hooks,
            clock,
            job_executor,
            module_loader,
            optimizer_options: OptimizerOptions::OPTIMIZE_ALL,
            root_shape,
            parser_identifier: 0,
            can_block: self.can_block,
            data: HostDefined::default(),
        };

        builtins::set_default_global_bindings(&mut context)?;

Now I have a good understanding of Context. Let me now design the implementation.

### Plan

1. Create a new module `core/engine/src/context/evaluation.rs` (or `core/engine/src/evaluation.rs`) defining:
   - `EvaluationHandle` struct (Clone, Debug, Trace, Finalize) wrapping `Gc<EvaluationInner>`.
   - `EvaluationInner` struct with `parent: Option<EvaluationHandle>` and `state: GcRefCell<EvaluationState>`.
   - `EvaluationState { cancelled: bool, reason: Option<JsValue> }` (reason `None` = cancelled without custom reason).
   - Methods:
     - `pub fn child(&self, context: &mut Context) -> EvaluationHandle` - creates child with parent = self clone.
     - `pub fn cancel(&self) -> bool`
     - `pub fn cancel_with_reason(&self, reason: impl Into<JsValue>) -> bool`
     - `pub fn is_cancelled(&self) -> bool`
     - `pub fn cancellation_reason(&self, context: &mut Context) -> Option<JsValue>`
   - internal helpers: `first_cancelled_ancestor` etc.

2. Add to `Context`:
   - field `active_evaluation: Vec<EvaluationHandle>` (stack) or `Option<EvaluationHandle>`. For nested evaluations, a stack. Actually let's use a
`Vec<EvaluationHandle>`.
   - `pub fn new_evaluation_handle(&self) -> EvaluationHandle`
   - `pub fn new_child_evaluation_handle(&self, parent: &EvaluationHandle) -> EvaluationHandle`
   - `pub fn eval_with_evaluation<R: ReadChar>(&mut self, source: Source<R>, handle: &EvaluationHandle) -> JsResult<JsValue>`
   - `pub fn enqueue_job_with_evaluation(&mut self, job: Job, handle: &EvaluationHandle) -> JsResult<()>`
   - `pub fn run_jobs_with_evaluation(&mut self, handle: &EvaluationHandle) -> JsResult<()>`
   - internal `with_evaluation(handle, f)` to push/pop the active handle stack.

3. Modify `Script`:
   - `pub fn evaluate_with_evaluation(&self, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<JsValue>`
   - Refactor `evaluate` to use common `prepare_run` etc.
   - In `evaluate_with_evaluation`, check handle cancellation before `prepare_run` (before user code runs). Then set active handle during run.

4. Modify `Module`:
   - `pub fn evaluate_with_evaluation(&self, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<JsPromise>`
   - `pub fn load_link_evaluate_with_evaluation(&self, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsPromise`
   - Need to check cancellation at phase boundaries. For `evaluate_with_evaluation`, if handle already cancelled: return Ok(rejected promise with
reason) (requirement 6). If cancelled during evaluation... how to reject with same reason? The `evaluate` may return an error; we should convert to a
rejected promise with the cancellation reason. Let's look at how `source_text_evaluate` returns errors and how promises are rejected.
   - `load_link_evaluate_with_evaluation`: returns a promise directly (not fallible). It should check cancellation at phase boundaries: if already
cancelled -> rejected promise. Then load phase, link phase, evaluate phase, each checks cancellation before proceeding. If cancellation happens after
load but before evaluate, the promise should reject with the cancellation reason and prevent side effects.

5. Modify VM:
   - Add cancellation checkpoints. Need a way to produce a `JsError`/`CompletionRecord` when cancelled.
   - The VM's `run` and `run_async_with_budget` should check `context`'s active evaluation handles for cancellation. The check could be every
instruction (expensive but correct) or every N instructions. Requirement: "Cancelling during script execution must stop before later side effects" -
so at least checkpoints between instructions. We can check every instruction; simple and correct. But there's a subtlety: if cancellation is triggered
from within a native function callback during execution, the VM loop will check at the next instruction and abort. That satisfies "stop before later
side effects" if "later" means after the checkpoint. But if a single JS statement compiles to multiple instructions, "later side effects" in the same
statement after the cancellation point would still run until the next instruction boundary. However the requirement likely means later statements. Let
me think about the test: e.g. script `globalThis.x = 1; while(true){}` or `console.log("before"); cancel(); console.log("after")`. With a checkpoint
at each instruction, cancel during a native call (e.g. a custom function that cancels) would cause the next instruction (after the call returns) to
abort. So `after` won't execute. Good.

   But wait - when cancellation happens during a native callback, the VM is inside `execute_one` for a `Call` opcode, which calls the native function.
After the native function returns (with `Ok`), the VM continues to the next instruction. The checkpoint at the top of the loop will detect
cancellation and abort. So the abort occurs at the instruction boundary. Good.

   Where to put the checkpoint: at the top of the `while let Some(byte) = ...` loop, before executing the instruction. If cancelled, return
`CompletionRecord::Throw(...)` or `Return`? We need to produce an error that propagates as `JsError` with the cancellation reason. Let's look at how
errors are represented.

6. Modify `Context::enqueue_job` to attach active handle to jobs automatically (requirement 10).
   - This requires a way to attach the handle to the job. We'll add a variant to `Job` or pass handle to executor. Let me think more carefully about
the executor API.

### Job association design

Let me reconsider. The `JobExecutor` trait:
```rust
pub trait JobExecutor: Any {
    fn enqueue_job(self: Rc<Self>, job: Job, context: &mut Context);
    fn run_jobs(self: Rc<Self>, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<()>;
    async fn run_jobs_async(self: Rc<Self>, context: &RefCell<&mut Context>) -> JsResult<()> { ... }
}
```

Option 1: Add a new variant `Job::WithEvaluation { handle: EvaluationHandle, job: Box<Job> }`. Then:
- `Context::enqueue_job` wraps with active handle if present.
- `Context::enqueue_job_with_evaluation` wraps with given handle.
- `SimpleJobExecutor::enqueue_job` matches new variant, extracts handle and inner job, stores both.
- `SimpleJobExecutor::run_jobs` checks handle before calling.

But external JobExecutor implementations would receive `Job::WithEvaluation` and have to handle it (with wildcard, they could just run the inner job
ignoring cancellation, or drop it). Since `Job` is non_exhaustive, external match must have wildcard. But the wildcard arm likely does nothing (or
errors?). Let's check existing external implementations in the repo. Search for `impl JobExecutor`.

impl JobExecutor for Executor {
    fn enqueue_job(self: Rc<Self>, job: Job, context: &mut Context) {
        match job {
            Job::PromiseJob(job) => self.promise_jobs.borrow_mut().push_back(job),
            Job::AsyncJob(job) => self.async_jobs.borrow_mut().push_back(job),
            Job::TimeoutJob(job) => {
                let now = context.clock().now();
                self.timeout_jobs
                    .borrow_mut()
                    .entry(now + job.timeout())
                    .or_default()
                    .push(job);
            }
            Job::GenericJob(job) => self.generic_jobs.borrow_mut().push_back(job),
            job => self.printer.print(format!("unsupported job type {job:?}")),
        }
    }

    fn run_jobs(self: Rc<Self>, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<()> {
        future::block_on(self.run_jobs_async(&RefCell::new(context)))
    }

    async fn run_jobs_async(self: Rc<Self>, context: &RefCell<&mut Context>) -> JsResult<()> {
        let mut group = FutureGroup::new();

        loop {
            for job in mem::take(&mut *self.async_jobs.borrow_mut()) {
                group.insert(job.call(context));
            }

            if let Some(Err(e)) = future::poll_once(group.next()).await.flatten() {
                self.printer.print(uncaught_job_error(&e));
            }

            // We particularly want to make this check here such that the
            // event loop is cancelled almost immediately after the channel with
            // the reader gets closed.
            if self.is_empty() && group.is_empty() {
                return Ok(());
            }

            {
                let context = &mut context.borrow_mut();
                self.drain_timeout_jobs(context);
                self.drain_generic_jobs(context);

                let jobs = mem::take(&mut *self.promise_jobs.borrow_mut());
                for job in jobs {
                    if let Err(e) = job.call(context) {
                        self.printer.print(uncaught_job_error(&e));
                    }
                }
            }
            context.borrow_mut().clear_kept_objects();

            future::yield_now().await;
        }
    }
}

    }
}

impl JobExecutor for Queue {
    fn enqueue_job(self: Rc<Self>, job: Job, context: &mut Context) {
        match job {
            Job::PromiseJob(job) => self.promise_jobs.borrow_mut().push_back(job),
            Job::AsyncJob(job) => self.async_jobs.borrow_mut().push_back(job),
            Job::TimeoutJob(t) => {
                let now = context.clock().now();
                self.timeout_jobs.borrow_mut().insert(now + t.timeout(), t);
            }
            Job::GenericJob(g) => self.generic_jobs.borrow_mut().push_back(g),
            _ => panic!("unsupported job type"),
        }
    }

    // While the sync flavor of `run_jobs` will block the current thread until all the jobs have finished...
    fn run_jobs(self: Rc<Self>, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<()> {
        let runtime = tokio::runtime::Builder::new_current_thread()
            .enable_time()
            .build()
            .unwrap();

        task::LocalSet::default().block_on(&runtime, self.run_jobs_async(&RefCell::new(context)))
    }

    // ...the async flavor won't, which allows concurrent execution with external async tasks.
    async fn run_jobs_async(self: Rc<Self>, context: &RefCell<&mut Context>) -> JsResult<()> {
        let mut group = FutureGroup::new();
        loop {
            for job in std::mem::take(&mut *self.async_jobs.borrow_mut()) {
                group.insert(job.call(context));
            }

            if group.is_empty()
                && self.promise_jobs.borrow().is_empty()
                && self.timeout_jobs.borrow().is_empty()
                && self.generic_jobs.borrow().is_empty()
            {
                // All queues are empty. We can exit.
                return Ok(());
            }

            // We have some jobs pending on the microtask queue. Try to poll the pending
            // tasks once to see if any of them finished, and run the pending microtasks
            // otherwise.
            if let Some(Err(err)) = future::poll_once(group.next()).await.flatten() {
                eprintln!("Uncaught {err}");
            };

            // Only one macrotask can be executed before the next drain of the microtask queue.

STEP 24 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
These external executors have a wildcard arm that prints or panics on unsupported job types. If we add a `Job::WithEvaluation` variant, those
executors would panic/print. That's undesirable for the feature to work with custom executors, but the requirements focus on `SimpleJobExecutor`
likely. Still, it would be nicer to not break custom executors.

Alternative design: Instead of adding a variant to `Job`, change the `JobExecutor` trait to add handle-aware methods, and store handle associations
inside the executor. For custom executors, the new methods can have defaults that just delegate to old ones (which would lose the cancellation
association, but at least not break). For `SimpleJobExecutor`, we implement properly.

But then how does `SimpleJobExecutor` store the association? It can store `(Job, Option<EvaluationHandle>)` in its queues. We'd modify
`SimpleJobExecutor` fields to hold `VecDeque<(PromiseJob, Option<EvaluationHandle>)>`, etc. And `run_jobs` skips jobs whose handle is cancelled.

But there's a problem: `Context::enqueue_job_with_evaluation` needs to pass the handle to the executor. If we keep the old `enqueue_job(job, context)`
method unchanged, we can't pass handle. So we add a new method to the trait:
```rust
fn enqueue_job_with_evaluation(self: Rc<Self>, job: Job, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) {
    self.enqueue_job(job, context); // default: ignore handle
}
```
But then `SimpleJobExecutor` overrides it to store the handle. Similarly for `run_jobs_with_evaluation`:
```rust
fn run_jobs_with_evaluation(self: Rc<Self>, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<()> {
    self.run_jobs(context)
}
```
`SimpleJobExecutor` overrides to set active handle and run with skip checks.

But there's a subtlety: `SimpleJobExecutor::enqueue_job` (plain) is called by `Context::enqueue_job` which should auto-associate the active handle.
Since `Context` knows the active handle, it could call `enqueue_job_with_evaluation` when there's an active handle. That's fine.

Now, what about `run_jobs` (plain)? It should still run all jobs, skipping cancelled ones (because jobs may have been enqueued with handles via
`enqueue_job_with_evaluation` even when not currently running under a handle). So `SimpleJobExecutor::run_jobs` needs to skip jobs with cancelled
handles regardless. This is fine.

But wait - `JobExecutor` trait is object-safe? It's used as `Rc<dyn JobExecutor>`. Adding methods with `self: Rc<Self>` and generic
`&EvaluationHandle` is fine. Default implementations require `Self: Sized`? No, default methods can be object-safe as long as they don't use `Self` in
a way that breaks object safety. A method with `self: Rc<Self>` and default body is object-safe. Let me verify: methods with `self: Rc<Self>` are
object-safe (they're not dispatchable via dyn because `Self: Sized`? Actually `self: Rc<Self>` is allowed in object-safe traits? Let me recall -
`self: Rc<Self>` methods are object-safe? The existing trait already has `fn enqueue_job(self: Rc<Self>, ...)` and is used as `Rc<dyn JobExecutor>`,
so yes it's object-safe. Default methods with `self: Rc<Self>` should also be object-safe.

But if we add a method `enqueue_job_with_evaluation(self: Rc<Self>, job: Job, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context)` with a default body,
it's object-safe.

Now the challenge: `SimpleJobExecutor` stores `(Job, Option<EvaluationHandle>)`. But the `Job` here is the inner concrete variant (e.g.,
`PromiseJob`), not the wrapper. We need to change the queue field types. This is internal to `SimpleJobExecutor`, so fine.

But there's a complication with `run_jobs` for the `SimpleJobExecutor`: when skipping a job whose handle is cancelled, we should NOT run it, but we
also shouldn't clear all jobs? Requirement 12: "started jobs may complete, while later not-yet-started jobs for the cancelled handle are skipped." So
on cancellation mid-drain, jobs already started (e.g., async jobs in group) may complete, later jobs for the cancelled handle are skipped (not run),
and jobs for other handles still run. So the drain loop should skip cancelled-handle jobs as it dequeues them.

Now, what about `run_jobs_with_evaluation(handle)`? Requirement 14: fail immediately if handle already cancelled, and not drain queued jobs in that
failed call. So the implementation:
```rust
pub fn run_jobs_with_evaluation(&mut self, handle: &EvaluationHandle) -> JsResult<()> {
    if handle.is_cancelled() {
        return Err(cancellation_error(handle, self));
    }
    // push active handle, call run_jobs (or executor's run_jobs_with_evaluation), pop
}
```
But if we call the executor's `run_jobs_with_evaluation`, the SimpleJobExecutor can skip cancelled jobs. The plain `run_jobs` also skips cancelled
jobs. The difference is just the immediate failure check. Actually maybe `run_jobs_with_evaluation` should also set the active handle so jobs spawned
during the drain are associated with that handle (requirement 10? not explicitly for run_jobs_with_evaluation, but makes sense). Let's have
`Context::run_jobs_with_evaluation` push the handle onto the active stack, call `self.job_executor().run_jobs(self)`, then pop. That way the
executor's plain run_jobs does the skipping.

But there's a subtlety: if we push active handle around `run_jobs`, then jobs spawned by running jobs under the active handle get auto-associated.
Good. And `run_jobs_with_evaluation` failing immediately when handle already cancelled: check before push/run.

Now, what does `Context::run_jobs_with_evaluation` do about only draining jobs associated with that handle vs all jobs? I think it drains all jobs
(the executor drains all queues). The handle mainly provides cancellation context. Requirement 14 only says it fails immediately if already cancelled
and doesn't drain in failed call. So draining all jobs is acceptable.

Now, let's reconsider the `Job` wrapper vs trait method approach.

Trait method approach:
- Add `enqueue_job_with_evaluation(self: Rc<Self>, job: Job, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context)` default -> calls `enqueue_job`.
- Add `run_jobs_with_evaluation(self: Rc<Self>, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<()>` default -> calls `run_jobs`.
- `SimpleJobExecutor` overrides both to store/check handle.

But there's a problem: `Context::enqueue_job` (plain) needs to auto-associate the active handle. It can do:
```rust
pub fn enqueue_job(&mut self, job: Job) {
    if let Some(handle) = self.active_evaluation_handle() {
        self.job_executor().enqueue_job_with_evaluation(job, handle, self);
    } else {
        self.job_executor().enqueue_job(job, self);
    }
}
```
And `enqueue_job_with_evaluation`:
```rust
pub fn enqueue_job_with_evaluation(&mut self, job: Job, handle: &EvaluationHandle) -> JsResult<()> {
    if handle.is_cancelled() {
        return Err(cancellation_error(handle, self));
    }
    self.job_executor().enqueue_job_with_evaluation(job, handle, self);
    Ok(())
}
```
Requirement 8: must fail immediately when handle already cancelled and must not enqueue that job. Good.

Now, for the SimpleJobExecutor, `enqueue_job_with_evaluation` stores `(job, Some(handle))`; `enqueue_job` stores `(job, None)`.

But wait - how do we store `EvaluationHandle` in the executor? `EvaluationHandle` is `!Send` (contains Gc). `SimpleJobExecutor` is stored in `Rc` and
jobs are `!Send`, and `run_jobs_async` is `!Send` (with `#[allow(clippy::future_not_send)]`). So fine.

Now `run_jobs` in SimpleJobExecutor: it dequeues `(PromiseJob, Option<EvaluationHandle>)`. Before calling, check `if let Some(handle) = &handle { if
handle.is_cancelled() { skip } }`. Also timeout jobs and async jobs.

But there's another subtlety: async jobs in `FutureGroup`. They are polled asynchronously. If a job is already started (inserted into group), it may
complete. Requirement 12 says started jobs may complete. So we shouldn't skip already-started async jobs. For not-yet-started queued async jobs (in
`async_jobs` queue), we should skip before inserting into group. Good.

Now, how does cancellation during job execution get detected? A job running under a handle (auto-associated) can call `cancel()` on the handle
(captured in closure), then continue executing JS. If the job is a JS microtask (PromiseJob) that evaluates a script, the VM checkpoints will detect
cancellation and abort. But if the job is a `NativeJob` that just runs native code, there's no VM checkpoint. That's fine - native code is host code
and can check the handle itself.

Now, the VM cancellation checkpoint needs to know the active handle. We'll add to `Context` a stack of active evaluation handles
(`Vec<EvaluationHandle>`), and a method `active_evaluation_handle()` returning the top. The VM's `run` loop can check
`context.is_current_evaluation_cancelled()` at each instruction. If cancelled, it should produce a cancellation error.

But wait - `Context::run()` is called by `Script::evaluate` and also by module evaluation etc. The VM's `run` is also called recursively for nested
calls? Let me check: `Context::run` is a method on `Context` that runs the current frame. It's called by `Script::evaluate` and
`Script::evaluate_with_evaluation`. Module evaluation probably calls `script.evaluate` or `Context::run` directly. Let me check source.rs.

            for required in requested {

                }
                // iii. If state.[[IsLoading]] is false, return unused.
                if !state.loading.get() {
                    return;
                }
            }
        }
    }

    /// Concrete method [`GetExportedNames ( [ exportStarSet ] )`][spec].
    ///
    /// [spec]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-getexportednames
    pub(super) fn get_exported_names(
        &self,
        module_self: &Module,
        export_star_set: &mut Vec<Module>,
        interner: &Interner,
    ) -> FxHashSet<JsString> {
        // 1. Assert: module.[[Status]] is not new.
        // 2. If exportStarSet is not present, set exportStarSet to a new empty List.

        // 3. If exportStarSet contains module, then
        if export_star_set.contains(module_self) {
            // a. Assert: We've reached the starting point of an export * circularity.
            // b. Return a new empty List.
            return FxHashSet::default();
        }

        // 4. Append module to exportStarSet.
        export_star_set.push(module_self.clone());

        // 5. Let exportedNames be a new empty List.
        let mut exported_names = FxHashSet::default();

        // 6. For each ExportEntry Record e of module.[[LocalExportEntries]], do
        for e in &self.code.local_export_entries {
            // a. Assert: module provides the direct binding for this export.
            // b. Append e.[[ExportName]] to exportedNames.
            let name = e.export_name().to_js_string(interner);
            exported_names.insert(name);
        }

        // 7. For each ExportEntry Record e of module.[[IndirectExportEntries]], do
        for e in &self.code.indirect_export_entries {
            // a. Assert: module imports a specific binding for this export.
            // b. Append e.[[ExportName]] to exportedNames.
            let name = e.export_name().to_js_string(interner);
            exported_names.insert(name);
        }

        // 8. For each ExportEntry Record e of module.[[StarExportEntries]], do
        for e in &self.code.star_export_entries {
            // a. Let requestedModule be GetImportedModule(module, e.[[ModuleRequest]]).
            let requested_module = self.loaded_modules.borrow()[e].clone();

            // b. Let starNames be requestedModule.GetExportedNames(exportStarSet).
            // c. For each element n of starNames, do
            for n in requested_module.get_exported_names(export_star_set, interner) {
                // i. If SameValue(n, "default") is false, then
                if n != js_str!("default") {
                    // 1. If exportedNames does not contain n, then
                    //    a. Append n to exportedNames.
                    exported_names.insert(n);
                }
            }
        }

        // 9. Return exportedNames.
        exported_names
    }

                    binding_name: BindingName::Name(e.local_name().to_js_string(interner)),
                });

    /// Concrete method [`Evaluate ( )`][spec].
    ///
    /// [spec]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-moduleevaluation
    pub(super) fn evaluate(
        &self,
        module_self: &Module,
        context: &mut Context,
    ) -> JsResult<JsPromise> {
        // 1. Assert: This call to Evaluate is not happening at the same time as another call to Evaluate within the surrounding agent.
        let (module, promise) = {
            match &*self.status.borrow() {
                ModuleStatus::Unlinked { .. }
                | ModuleStatus::Linking { .. }
                | ModuleStatus::PreLinked { .. }
                | ModuleStatus::Evaluating { .. } => {
                    unreachable!(
                        "2. Assert: module.[[Status]] is one of linked, evaluating-async, or evaluated."
                    )
                }
                ModuleStatus::Linked { .. } => (module_self.clone(), None),
                // 3. If module.[[Status]] is either evaluating-async or evaluated, set module to module.[[CycleRoot]].
                ModuleStatus::EvaluatingAsync {
                    cycle_root,
                    top_level_capability,
                    ..
                }
                | ModuleStatus::Evaluated {
                    cycle_root,
                    top_level_capability,
                    ..
                } => (
                    cycle_root.clone(),
                    top_level_capability
                        .as_ref()
                        .map(|cap| {
                            JsPromise::from_object(cap.promise().clone()).js_expect(
                                "promise created from the %Promise% intrinsic is always native",
                            )
                        })
                        .transpose()?,
                ),
            }
        };

        // 4. If module.[[TopLevelCapability]] is not empty, then
        if let Some(promise) = promise {
            // a. Return module.[[TopLevelCapability]].[[Promise]].
            return Ok(promise);
        }

        // 5. Let stack be a new empty List.
        let mut stack = Vec::new();

        // 6. Let capability be ! NewPromiseCapability(%Promise%).
        // 7. Set module.[[TopLevelCapability]] to capability.
        let capability = PromiseCapability::new(
            &context.intrinsics().constructors().promise().constructor(),
            context,
        )
        .js_expect(
            "capability creation must always succeed when using the `%Promise%` intrinsic",
        )?;

        // 8. Let result be Completion(InnerModuleEvaluation(module, stack, 0)).
        let ModuleKind::SourceText(module_src) = module.kind() else {
            unreachable!("module must be a source text module");
        };
        let result =
            module_src.inner_evaluate(&module, &mut stack, 0, Some(capability.clone()), context);

        match result {
            Ok(_) => {

                assert!(match &*module_src.status.borrow() {
                    ModuleStatus::EvaluatingAsync { .. } => true,

                    ModuleStatus::Evaluated { error, .. } if error.is_none() => true,
                    _ => false,
                });

                if matches!(&*module_src.status.borrow(), ModuleStatus::Evaluated { .. }) {

                    //    ii. Perform ! Call(capability.[[Resolve]], undefined, « undefined »).
                    capability
                        .resolve()

                        .js_expect("cannot fail for the default resolve function")?;
                }

                //     d. Assert: stack is empty.
                assert!(stack.is_empty());

            Err(err) => {

                for m in stack.iter().filter_map(|cmr| cmr.kind().as_source_text()) {

                        } => {

                            // iii. Set m.[[EvaluationError]] to result.
                            ModuleStatus::Evaluated {
                                environment,
                                top_level_capability,
                                cycle_root,
                                error: Some(err.clone()),

core/engine/src/vm/completion_record.rs:14:pub(crate) enum CompletionRecord {
//! An implementation of a `CompletionRecord` for Boa's VM.

#![allow(clippy::inline_always)]

use crate::{Context, JsError, JsResult, JsValue};
use boa_gc::{Finalize, Trace, custom_trace};
use std::ops::ControlFlow;

/// An implementation of the ECMAScript's `CompletionRecord` [specification] for
/// Boa's VM output Completion and Result.
///
/// [specification]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-completion-record-specification-type
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Finalize)]
pub(crate) enum CompletionRecord {
    Normal(JsValue),
    Return(JsValue),
    Throw(JsError),
}

// SAFETY: this matches all possible variants and traces
// their inner contents, which makes this safe.
unsafe impl Trace for CompletionRecord {
    custom_trace!(this, mark, {
        match this {
            Self::Normal(v) => mark(v),
            Self::Return(r) => mark(r),
            Self::Throw(th) => mark(th),
        }
    });
}

// ---- `CompletionRecord` methods ----
impl CompletionRecord {
    pub(crate) const fn is_throw_completion(&self) -> bool {
        matches!(self, Self::Throw(_))
    }

    /// This function will consume the current `CompletionRecord` and return a `JsResult<JsValue>`
    // NOTE: rustc bug around evaluating destructors that prevents this from being a const function.
    // Related issue(s):
    //   - https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/issues/4041
    //   - https://github.com/rust-lang/rust/issues/60964
    //   - https://github.com/rust-lang/rust/issues/73255
    #[allow(clippy::missing_const_for_fn)]
    pub(crate) fn consume(self) -> JsResult<JsValue> {
        match self {
            Self::Throw(error) => Err(error),
            Self::Normal(value) | Self::Return(value) => Ok(value),
        }
    }
}

pub(crate) trait IntoCompletionRecord {
    fn into_completion_record(self, context: &mut Context) -> ControlFlow<CompletionRecord>;
}

impl IntoCompletionRecord for () {
    #[inline(always)]
    fn into_completion_record(self, _: &mut Context) -> ControlFlow<CompletionRecord> {
        ControlFlow::Continue(())
    }
}

impl IntoCompletionRecord for JsError {
    #[inline(always)]
    fn into_completion_record(self, context: &mut Context) -> ControlFlow<CompletionRecord> {
        context.handle_error(self)
    }
}

impl IntoCompletionRecord for JsResult<()> {
    #[inline(always)]
    fn into_completion_record(self, context: &mut Context) -> ControlFlow<CompletionRecord> {
        match self {
            Ok(()) => ControlFlow::Continue(()),
            Err(err) => context.handle_error(err),
        }
    }
}

Now let's think about the exact VM checkpoint. In `Context::run`, the loop:

We can add at the top of the loop:
```rust

    let err = self.evaluation_cancellation_error(); // builds JsError from active handle reason
```

But we need to be careful: `Context::run` is called for every script/module execution, even when not under an evaluation handle. In that case
`is_evaluation_cancelled()` should return false (no active handle). Good.

However, there's a subtle issue: The active handle stack. When `Script::evaluate_with_evaluation` runs, it pushes the handle. Inside, JS code may call
a native function that calls `context.eval_with_evaluation(child_handle, ...)` nested. That nested eval pushes child handle, runs, pops. The outer
active handle remains. The checkpoint checks the top of the stack (or all active handles). If any active handle is cancelled, abort. Since parent
cancellation cascades to children semantically (is_cancelled walks ancestors), checking just the top child would detect parent cancellation too (if
child is a descendant). But if nested eval uses an unrelated handle, checking all active handles is safer - e.g., outer handle A cancelled, inner uses
unrelated B - the checkpoint should probably still abort because the outer evaluation is cancelled. I'll check all active handles in the stack.
Actually simpler: check `self.active_evaluations.iter().any(|h| h.is_cancelled())`.

But we also need `run_jobs_with_evaluation` to push the handle, so jobs run under it and spawned jobs are associated. That fits the same stack
mechanism.

Now, about `Context::run` being called recursively within a single eval (nested host calls). The active handle stack is on Context, so nested `run()`
calls see it. Good.

mention async variants. However, to be safe, add checkpoints to both `run` and `run_async_with_budget`. Actually, for `run_async_with_budget`, the
check at the top of the loop would also work.

plain `evaluate` inherit the active handle? Requirement 10 is about jobs; requirement about nested evaluations: "cancellation across nested
evaluations". It probably makes sense that a plain eval called while under an active handle should continue under that handle (since Context has the
active stack). So `Script::evaluate` should NOT push/pop; it just runs under whatever active handles exist. Good. Only `evaluate_with_evaluation`

    /// Abstract method [`LoadRequestedModules ( [ hostDefined ] )`][spec].
    ///
    /// Prepares the module for linking by loading all its module dependencies. Returns a `JsPromise`
    /// that will resolve when the loading process either completes or fails.
    ///
    /// [spec]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#table-abstract-methods-of-module-records
    #[allow(clippy::missing_panics_doc)]
    #[inline]
    pub fn load(&self, context: &mut Context) -> JsPromise {
        match self.kind() {
            ModuleKind::SourceText(_) => {
                // Concrete method [`LoadRequestedModules ( [ hostDefined ] )`][spec].
                //
                // [spec]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-LoadRequestedModules
                // 1. If hostDefined is not present, let hostDefined be empty.

                // 2. Let pc be ! NewPromiseCapability(%Promise%).
                let pc = PromiseCapability::new(

                    context,

                );

                    //     [[IsLoading]]: true, [[PendingModulesCount]]: 1, [[Visited]]: « »,
                    //     [[PromiseCapability]]: pc, [[HostDefined]]: hostDefined
                    // }.
                    &Rc::new(GraphLoadingState {
                        capability: pc.clone(),
                        loading: Cell::new(true),
                        pending_modules: Cell::new(1),
                        visited: RefCell::default(),
                    }),
                    context,
                );

                // 5. Return pc.[[Promise]].
                JsPromise::from_object(pc.promise().clone())
                    .expect("promise created from the %Promise% intrinsic is always native")
            }
            ModuleKind::Synthetic(_) => SyntheticModule::load(context),
        }
    }

So `Module::load` returns a `JsPromise` directly. `load_link_evaluate` chains `.then(link).then(evaluate)`.

For `load_link_evaluate_with_evaluation`, we can build a similar chain but with cancellation checks in each `.then` callback, and with the handle
captured so jobs spawned are associated.

Let me think about how to run the load/link/evaluate phases under the handle. Since the `.then` callbacks are jobs (they'll run during `run_jobs`),
and jobs spawned by code running under a handle are auto-associated, we need to ensure that when the callback runs, the active handle is set. We can
push the handle inside the callback:
```rust
NativeFunction::from_copy_closure_with_captures(
    |_, _, (handle, module), context| {
        context.push_evaluation(handle.clone());
        let result = ...;
        context.pop_evaluation();
        result
    },
    (handle.clone(), self.clone()),
)
```
But `handle` is `EvaluationHandle` which is `Trace`, so it can be captured in `from_copy_closure_with_captures`. Good.

However, there's a subtlety: The `.then` callbacks receive the previous promise's value, and if the previous promise was rejected, the `on_fulfilled`
isn't called. So if load rejects (e.g., due to cancellation), the subsequent `.then` callbacks won't run. Good.

For phase-boundary cancellation: we need to check before link and before evaluate. So:
```rust
pub fn load_link_evaluate_with_evaluation(&self, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsPromise {
    if handle.is_cancelled() {
        let reason = handle.cancellation_reason(context).unwrap_or_else(...);
        return JsPromise::rejected(reason, context).expect(...);
    }
    self.load(context)
        .then(
            Some(NativeFunction::from_copy_closure_with_captures(
                |_, _, (handle, module), context| {
                    if handle.is_cancelled() {
                        return Err(JsError::from_opaque(handle.cancellation_reason(context).unwrap_or_else(...)));
                    }
                    let result = context.with_evaluation(handle, |context| module.link(context));
                    result.map(|_| JsValue::undefined())
                },
                (handle.clone(), self.clone()),
            ).to_js_function(context.realm())),
            None,
            context,
        )
        .expect(...)
        .then(
            Some(NativeFunction::from_copy_closure_with_captures(
                |_, _, (handle, module), context| {
                    if handle.is_cancelled() {
                        return Err(JsError::from_opaque(...));
                    }
                    context.with_evaluation(handle, |context| {
                        Ok(module.evaluate(context)?.into())
                    })
                },
                (handle.clone(), self.clone()),
            ).to_js_function(context.realm())),
            None,
            context,
        )
        .expect(...)
}
```
This handles phase boundaries: if cancelled after load but before link, link callback returns Err (rejects promise). If cancelled after link but
before evaluate, evaluate callback returns Err. And the rejection value is the cancellation reason (since `Err(JsError::from_opaque(reason))` ->
promise rejects with `reason`).

But wait: requirement 6 says "Module::evaluate_with_evaluation and Module::load_link_evaluate_with_evaluation must reject with the same cancellation
reason value that cancelled the handle." The `.then` callbacks returning `Err(JsError::from_opaque(reason))` will cause the promise chain to reject
with that reason. Good.

Also, when `module.evaluate(context)` runs under the handle and cancellation happens during evaluation, it returns `Ok(rejected promise)` whose
rejection is the cancellation reason. The `.then` callback returns `Ok(promise.into())`; the outer promise adopts the inner promise's state. So the
final promise is rejected with the same reason. Good.

But there's a subtlety with `context.with_evaluation` - if I add a helper on Context that pushes/pops. Let me define:
```rust
pub(crate) fn with_evaluation<T>(&mut self, handle: &EvaluationHandle, f: impl FnOnce(&mut Self) -> T) -> T {
    self.push_evaluation(handle.clone());
    let result = f(self);
    self.pop_evaluation();
    result
}
```
But `f` takes `&mut Self` where Self = Context. The closure `|context| module.link(context)` - here context is `&mut Context`. Fine.

```rust
pub fn evaluate_with_evaluation(&self, handle: &EvaluationHandle, context: &mut Context) -> JsResult<JsValue> {
    if handle.is_cancelled() {
```

Now, `SimpleJobExecutor::run_jobs_async` needs to skip jobs whose handle is cancelled. Let me write the updated loop carefully.

Current:
```rust
async fn run_jobs_async(self: Rc<Self>, context: &RefCell<&mut Context>) -> JsResult<()> {
    let mut group = FutureGroup::new();
    loop {
        if self.stop... { ... }

        for job in mem::take(&mut *self.async_jobs.borrow_mut()) {
            group.insert(job.call(context));
        }
        // timeout dispatch
        ...
        if self.is_empty() && group.is_empty() { break; }
        if let Some(Err(err)) = future::poll_once(group.next()).await.flatten() { ... }
        let jobs = mem::take(&mut *self.promise_jobs.borrow_mut());
        for job in jobs { if let Err(err) = job.call(...) { ... } }
        let jobs = mem::take(&mut *self.generic_jobs.borrow_mut());
        for job in jobs { ... }
    }
}
```

We need to modify:
- `is_empty` to check the tuple queues.
- When taking async jobs: `for (job, handle) in ... { if handle.map_or(false, |h| h.is_cancelled()) { continue; } group.insert(job.call(context)); }`
- Timeout jobs: filter out cancelled handles.
- Promise jobs: skip cancelled handles.
- Generic jobs: skip cancelled handles.

For timeout jobs, the current dispatch uses `split_off(&now)` and retains not-cancelled. We need to also check handle cancelled. We can do
`jobs.retain(|(job, handle)| !job.is_cancelled() && handle.as_ref().map_or(false, |h| h.is_cancelled()) == false)`. Actually we want to keep jobs that
are not cancelled AND (handle is None OR handle not cancelled). Let me write helper `should_run(handle: &Option<EvaluationHandle>) -> bool`.

Now let's think about how `cancellation_reason` returns the reason for a handle. We need:
```rust
pub fn cancellation_reason(&self, context: &mut Context) -> Option<JsValue> {
    let mut current = Some(self.clone());
    while let Some(h) = current {
        let state = h.inner.state.borrow();
        if state.cancelled {
            return Some(state.reason.clone().unwrap_or_else(|| make_abort_error(context)));
        }
        // avoid borrowing parent while holding state borrow? Need to clone parent first.
        let parent = h.inner.parent.clone();
        drop(state);
        current = parent;
    }
    None
}
```
But `make_abort_error(context)` needs `&mut Context`, and `borrow` is immutable. We can clone the reason or clone the handle's own state first, then
call. Let me structure:
```rust
pub fn cancellation_reason(&self, context: &mut Context) -> Option<JsValue> {
    if let Some(reason) = self.first_effective_reason() {
        return Some(reason);
    }
    None
}
fn first_effective_reason(&self) -> Option<JsValue> {
    // walk up; for each node, if cancelled, return reason (may need context to build abort error)
}
```
Problem: building AbortError needs context. So we need to return either a stored reason or a marker "cancelled without reason" and construct later.
Let's return an enum:
```rust
enum ReasonSource {
    Value(JsValue),
    Abort,
}
```
Or simply: walk up to find the first cancelled node, clone its `reason: Option<JsValue>`, then outside borrow, if `Some(v)` return `Some(v)`, if
`None` return `Some(make_abort_error(context))`. Let's implement:

```rust
pub fn cancellation_reason(&self, context: &mut Context) -> Option<JsValue> {
    let mut current = Some(self.clone());
    while let Some(h) = current {
        let state = h.inner.state.borrow();
        if state.cancelled {
            let reason = state.reason.clone();
            drop(state);
            return Some(reason.unwrap_or_else(|| make_abort_error(context)));
        }
        let parent = h.inner.parent.clone();
        drop(state);
        current = parent;
    }
    None
}
```
This borrows `h.inner.state` then clones parent while borrowed? `h.inner.parent` is a different field, so `h.inner.parent.clone()` while `state` is
borrowed is fine (immutable borrows). Then `drop(state)` before calling `make_abort_error` which needs `&mut Context`; we don't hold any borrow at
that point. But `current` still holds `h` clone and `parent` - fine.

Actually, can we call `make_abort_error(context)` while `current` holds a `Gc` (immutable)? Yes, context is a separate object. But wait,
`make_abort_error` is a function taking `&mut Context`; `current` doesn't borrow context. Fine.

Now `is_cancelled`:
```rust
pub fn is_cancelled(&self) -> bool {
    let mut current = Some(self.clone());
    while let Some(h) = current {
        if h.inner.state.borrow().cancelled {
            return true;
        }
        let parent = h.inner.parent.clone();
        current = parent;
    }
    false
}
```

Now `cancel` and `cancel_with_reason`. We need to atomically check the lineage and set own state if no ancestor cancelled. But since it's
single-threaded, we can:
```rust
pub fn cancel(&self) -> bool {
    self.cancel_with_reason_impl(None)
}
pub fn cancel_with_reason(&self, reason: impl Into<JsValue>) -> bool {
    self.cancel_with_reason_impl(Some(reason.into()))
}
fn cancel_with_reason_impl(&self, reason: Option<JsValue>) -> bool {
    // If already cancelled (self or ancestor), return false.
    if self.is_cancelled() {
        return false;
    }
    let mut state = self.inner.state.borrow_mut();
    // Re-check after acquiring mutable borrow (no other thread, but safe)
    if state.cancelled {
        return false;
    }
    state.cancelled = true;
    state.reason = reason;
    true
}
```
Wait, but `is_cancelled` walks ancestors. If self not cancelled but ancestor is, returns true -> cancel returns false. Good. If self is already
cancelled, returns true -> false. If neither, we set. Since single-threaded, no race. But there is a subtle case: `is_cancelled()` returns false, then
between the check and borrow_mut, could something change? Single-threaded, no. But if a native callback calls `cancel` while a GC collection is
happening? No. Fine.

But there's a concern: `cancel_with_reason` takes `impl Into<JsValue>`, and `JsValue` is `Trace`? `JsValue` is `Trace`. Good. The reason stored in
`GcRefCell<Option<JsValue>>` will be traced. Good.

Now, there's a subtlety with `cancel_with_reason` and first-wins when child has own cancellation but ancestor later cancels: If child C has own reason
R1 and is cancelled. Parent P later `cancel_with_reason(R2)`. P is not cancelled (its own state), so `cancel_with_reason_impl` checks `is_cancelled()`
which walks ancestors - P has no ancestors, and P itself not cancelled -> false -> proceeds to set P's state. Returns true. So P's first effective
cancellation is R2. Good. C keeps R1. Good.

But what about: C is not cancelled, P is cancelled. C.cancel() -> `is_cancelled()` true -> returns false. C doesn't get own state. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: `cancel()` with no reason stores `reason: None`. Later `cancellation_reason` returns AbortError. But what if
`cancel_with_reason` is called after `cancel()` on the same handle? `is_cancelled` returns true -> returns false, reason stays None. Good (first
wins).

    /// Creates a `JsPromise` that is rejected with the reason `error`.
    ///
    /// Equivalent to the [`Promise.reject`] static method.
    ///
    /// `JsPromise::reject` is pretty similar to [`JsPromise::resolve`], with the difference that
    /// it always wraps `error` into a rejected promise, even if `error` is a promise or a [thenable].
    ///
    /// # Examples

    /// let error = JsError::from_opaque(5.0f64.into());
    ///
    /// assert!(error.as_opaque().is_some());
    /// ```
    #[must_use]
    pub fn from_opaque(value: JsValue) -> Self {
        // Recover the backtrace from the Error object if present,
        // so it survives the JsError → JsValue → JsError round-trip.
        let backtrace = value.as_object().and_then(|obj| {
            let error = obj.downcast_ref::<Error>()?;
            error.backtrace.0.clone()
        });
        Self {
            inner: Repr::Opaque(value),
            backtrace,
        }
    }

`JsPromise::reject` takes `E: Into<JsError>`. We can use `JsError::from_opaque(reason)`.

Now let me think about the module `evaluate_with_evaluation` for the already-cancelled case:
```rust
if handle.is_cancelled() {
    let reason = handle.cancellation_reason(context).unwrap_or_else(|| make_abort_error(context));
    return JsPromise::reject(JsError::from_opaque(reason), context).map_err(Into::into);
}
```
Wait signature returns `JsResult<JsPromise>`. So `return Ok(JsPromise::reject(JsError::from_opaque(reason), context)?);`

For `load_link_evaluate_with_evaluation` already-cancelled: `JsPromise::reject(JsError::from_opaque(reason), context).expect(...)`.

Now, let's consider how to share `make_abort_error` between runtime abort module and the new evaluation module. The runtime's `make_abort_error` is
private to that module. I'll create a similar function in the evaluation module. It's fine to duplicate (both create AbortError). But maybe we could
make a shared helper in engine. I'll create it in the evaluation module as `pub(crate) fn make_abort_error(context: &mut Context) -> JsValue`.

Now, let's also consider: `cancellation_reason(context)` when cancelled without reason must produce Error-like value whose string contains AbortError.
Our `make_abort_error` creates `JsNativeError::error().with_message("signal is aborted without reason").into_opaque(context)` and sets name
"AbortError". Its `to_string` would be "AbortError: signal is aborted without reason". Good.

Now, let's think about where to place `EvaluationHandle`. I'll create `core/engine/src/context/evaluation.rs` and export it from `context/mod.rs` as
`pub use evaluation::EvaluationHandle;` plus perhaps re-export at root via `prelude`? The required public entry points say `EvaluationHandle::{...}`.
It should be accessible as `boa_engine::context::EvaluationHandle` or `boa_engine::EvaluationHandle`. The task says "Public entry points must include
... `EvaluationHandle::{...}`". Probably they mean the type should be exported from the crate root. Let me add it to `prelude` and `context` module.
Actually `context` module is `pub mod context`, and `Context` is re-exported at root via prelude. I'll export `EvaluationHandle` from `context` module
and also from root prelude.

Let me check what other context types are exported at root. `Context` is in `prelude`. `HostHooks`, `Clock` are only in `context` module. For ease,
I'll add `EvaluationHandle` to both `context` module (pub use) and root `prelude`.

Now, let's think about the `Context` field initialization. Add `active_evaluations: Vec<EvaluationHandle>` (or `evaluation_stack`). Need to add to
Debug (can skip or add as count). Add to `ContextBuilder::build` initialization.

Now, about `Context::new_child_evaluation_handle` signature. Requirement says `Context::{new_evaluation_handle, new_child_evaluation_handle, ...}`.
`new_child_evaluation_handle(&self, parent: &EvaluationHandle) -> EvaluationHandle`. I'll implement `parent.child()`.

Now let's also think about whether `Context::new_evaluation_handle` should take `&self` or `&mut self`. Requirement lists it among Context entry
points but doesn't specify argument order for it. Since it doesn't mutate Context, `&self` is fine. But `Gc::new` might trigger GC which might need
`&mut Context`? `Gc::new` just allocates; GC collection might run and trace roots, but that's handled by the GC machinery, not requiring Context.
Let's check - does `Gc::new` require a Context? `Allocator::alloc_gc` might trigger collection which needs to find roots. Roots are tracked by
`Context` via `kept_alive` and the stack. But it doesn't take `&mut Context` as a parameter. Boa's GC is mostly a mark-sweep with roots collected from
the native stack? Actually looking at `Allocator::alloc_gc`, it may run the collector which traces all `Gc` pointers on the stack. Let me not worry -
`Gc::new` is called throughout the code without Context (e.g., `Gc::new` in many places). It's fine.

Now, let's also think about whether the `EvaluationHandle` should be `Copy`. No, it contains `Gc` which is Copy actually? `Gc<T>` implements Clone but
is it Copy? Let me check. If `Gc` is Copy, we could make EvaluationHandle Copy. But we need `Trace` etc. Let me just derive Clone; Copy not required.

//! Boa's implementation of the ECMAScript's module system.
//!
//! This module contains the [`Module`] type, which represents an [**Abstract Module Record**][module],
//! a [`ModuleLoader`] trait for custom module loader implementations, and [`SimpleModuleLoader`],
//! the default `ModuleLoader` for [`Context`] which can be used for most simple usecases.
//!
//! Every module roughly follows the same lifecycle:
//! - Parse using [`Module::parse`].
//! - Load all its dependencies using [`Module::load`].
//! - Link its dependencies together using [`Module::link`].
//! - Evaluate the module and its dependencies using [`Module::evaluate`].
//!
//! The [`ModuleLoader`] trait allows customizing the "load" step on the lifecycle
//! of a module, which allows doing things like fetching modules from urls, having multiple
//! "modpaths" from where to import modules, or using Rust futures to avoid blocking the main thread
//! on loads.
//!
//! More information:
//!  - [ECMAScript reference][spec]
//!
//! [spec]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-modules
//! [module]: https://tc39.es/ecma262/#sec-abstract-module-records

`EvaluationHandle` methods:

```rust
impl EvaluationHandle {
    pub(crate) fn new() -> Self {
        Self {
            inner: Gc::new(EvaluationInner {
                parent: None,
                state: GcRefCell::new(EvaluationState::default()),
            }),
        }
    }

    pub fn child(&self) -> Self {
        Self {
            inner: Gc::new(EvaluationInner {
                parent: Some(self.clone()),
                state: GcRefCell::new(EvaluationState::default()),
            }),
        }
    }

    pub fn cancel(&self) -> bool {
        self.cancel_with_opt_reason(None)
    }

    pub fn cancel_with_reason(&self, reason: impl Into<JsValue>) -> bool {
        self.cancel_with_opt_reason(Some(reason.into()))
    }

    fn cancel_with_opt_reason(&self, reason: Option<JsValue>) -> bool {
        if self.is_cancelled() {
            return false;
        }
        let mut state = self.inner.state.borrow_mut();
        if state.cancelled {
            return false;
        }
        state.cancelled = true;
        state.reason = reason;
        true
    }

    pub fn is_cancelled(&self) -> bool {
        let mut current = Some(self.clone());
        while let Some(handle) = current {
            if handle.inner.state.borrow().cancelled {
                return true;
            }
            current = handle.inner.parent.clone();
        }
        false
    }

    pub fn cancellation_reason(&self, context: &mut Context) -> Option<JsValue> {
        let mut current = Some(self.clone());
        while let Some(handle) = current {
            let state = handle.inner.state.borrow();
            if state.cancelled {
                let reason = state.reason.clone();
                drop(state);
                return Some(reason.unwrap_or_else(|| make_abort_error(context)));
            }
            current = handle.inner.parent.clone();
            drop(state);
        }
        None
    }
}
```

Wait, there's a borrow issue in `cancellation_reason`: `current = handle.inner.parent.clone()` while `state` borrow is alive. `handle.inner.parent` is
a separate field of the same `Gc<EvaluationInner>`, but `handle` is a `Gc` and we can access `handle.inner.parent` while `handle.inner.state` is
borrowed. That's fine (two immutable borrows of different fields). Then `drop(state)` is redundant. Let me just write:
```rust
let state = handle.inner.state.borrow();
if state.cancelled {
    let reason = state.reason.clone();
    // drop state
    return Some(reason.unwrap_or_else(|| make_abort_error(context)));
}
current = handle.inner.parent.clone();
```
Here `state` is dropped when we return or at end of loop iteration. But `make_abort_error(context)` needs `&mut Context` while `state` (borrow of
handle.inner.state) is still alive - but `handle` is a `Gc` not borrowing `context`, so no conflict with `context`. `make_abort_error` takes `&mut
Context`, and we have `&EvaluationHandle` (immutable), no borrow of context. So it's fine even with `state` alive. Actually `context` is `&mut
Context` parameter; `handle` doesn't borrow context. So no borrow conflict. Good.

But wait, inside the closure `reason.unwrap_or_else(|| make_abort_error(context))`, `context` is `&mut Context`, and `reason` is a local
`Option<JsValue>`. Fine.
